library(factoextra)

Import the Dataset of GSE11877 (pediatric ALL)

# Download the phenotypic data corresponding to the study
pheno <- getGEO("GSE11877")[[1]]
Found 1 file(s)
GSE11877_series_matrix.txt.gz
# Create an affybatch object to store the pheno and CEL file
#data. First make sure the pData sample names match the CEL file
#names to be read in and only read in CEL files for which pheno
#data are available

pheno <- pData(pheno)

# Read the CEL filenames
celfiles <- list.celfiles()

# extract first 9 chars to GSM ID
short.celfiles <- substr(celfiles,1,9)

# Keep only samples with CEL files
pheno <- pheno[rownames(pheno) %in% short.celfiles,]

# # confirm perfect match
all.equal(short.celfiles, rownames(pheno))
[1] TRUE
# Only because this returns TRUE we can execute the next two lines

# If the all.equal check is FALSE, be sure to sort pheno before relabeling the rownames

rownames(pheno) <- celfiles

# Divide the WBC count to two groups(High/Low)
pheno <- pheno |>
  mutate(wbc = as.numeric(sub(".*: ", "", characteristics_ch1.4))) |> 
  mutate(
    wbc_group = if_else(wbc >= 50, "High WBC", "Low WBC")
  )

affybatch <- ReadAffy(filenames = rownames(pheno), phenoData = pheno)

# Now you can proceed to pre-processing steps then analysis
# Check number of samples in each group
table(pheno$wbc_group)

High WBC  Low WBC 
     108       99

Pre-processing

1.QC on raw data

# Boxplot of raw probe intensities
boxplot(affybatch,
        main = "Raw Affymetrix probe intensities (GSE11877)",
        las = 2)
Warning: replacing previous import 'AnnotationDbi::tail' by 'utils::tail' when
loading 'hgu133plus2cdf'
Warning: replacing previous import 'AnnotationDbi::head' by 'utils::head' when
loading 'hgu133plus2cdf'

The raw Affymetrix boxplot shows a lot of variation across samples. For example, the medians are not lined up, and some arrays have clearly higher or lower intensity ranges than others. Since these differences are not biological, the data are not on the same scale yet. Therefore, it means normalization is needed to make the arrays comparable.

2.RMA Normalization (background correction, quantile normalization, probe summarization, log2 transformation)

all.rma <- rma(affybatch)
Background correcting
Normalizing
Calculating Expression
# QC check on probe intensities after RMA normalization
boxplot(exprs(all.rma),
        main = "RMA normalized log2 expression",
        las = 2)

After applying RMA normalization, the boxplot shows that all samples now have very similar distributions. The medians line up across all arrays, and the spreads (IQRs) are almost the same. This means the technical differences seen in the raw data have been corrected. The arrays are now placed on a common log2 scale, and the expression ranges look much more consistent.

3.Filtering

# Filter low-expressed probes
all.expr <- exprs(all.rma)

gene_means <- rowMeans(all.expr)
all.expr.mean.filt <- all.expr[gene_means > 4, ]

# Filter low-variance probes
gene_vars <- apply(all.expr.mean.filt, 1, var)
expr_final <- all.expr.mean.filt[gene_vars > median(gene_vars), ]

# Final ExpressionSet
exprset_final <- ExpressionSet(
  assayData = expr_final,
  phenoData = phenoData(all.rma)
)
# annotation(exprset_final) <- annotation(all.rma)

Dimentional Reductoin

1.Prepare PCA matrix

# Extract filtered expression matrix
expr_mat <- exprs(exprset_final)

# Transpose so rows represent samples
expr_t <- t(expr_mat)

# Run PCA
pca.sample <- prcomp(expr_t, scale. = TRUE)

2. Scree plot

fviz_eig(pca.sample)
Warning in geom_bar(stat = "identity", fill = barfill, color = barcolor, :
Ignoring empty aesthetic: `width`.

The scree plot shows that PC1 captures the largest share of variation (about 24.5%), with PC2 explaining another 12.3%.

3. PCA Scatterplot (PC1 vs. PC2)

wbc_group <- pData(exprset_final)$wbc_group

plot(pca.sample$x[, 1:2], col = as.factor(wbc_group),
     cex = 0.5, pch=ifelse(wbc_group=="High WBC",1,3),
     main = "PCA: PC1 vs PC2")
legend("topright",
       legend = levels(as.factor(wbc_group)),
       col = 1:length(levels(as.factor(wbc_group))),
       pch = c(1, 3))   # must match the pch used in plot()

When reducing the gene expression data to two dimensions, the plot did not help one identify subjects with high WBC versus low WBC. The PCA plot shows that High-WBC and Low-WBC samples mostly overlap in the first two principal components, meaning WBC level is not the main source of variation in the gene-expression data. There is only a slight shift along PC2, suggesting mild but not strong expression differences between the two groups.

Summary: The scree plot shows that PC1 and PC2 only explain a moderate amount of variation (24.5% and 12.3%). In the PCA scatter plot, the high-WBC and low-WBC groups overlap a lot, which tells me that WBC level is not driving the main expression patterns across all genes. This also makes sense biologically, since only certain pathways are expected to differ by WBC, not the whole transcriptome.There is a small shift along PC2, so there might still be meaningful gene-level differences, even though the global separation is weak.

Clustering

1.Hierarchical Clustering

# Compute distance matrix
dist_samples <- dist(t(expr_mat), method = "euclidean")

# Apply single linkage hierarchical clustering to the Euclidean distances plot the resulting dendrogram
hcl.complete <- hclust(dist_samples, method = "complete")

#  Plot the resulting dendrogram
plot(hcl.complete,
     label = FALSE,
     main = "Hierarchical Clustering of Samples")

The tree shows very bushy with no clear separation into two major branches. This means the high-WBC and low-WBC samples do not form two strong, distinct clusters based on global gene expression.

library(cluster)

set.seed(42)
gap_stat <- clusGap(t(expr_mat), FUN = kmeans, K.max = 10, B = 20)

# Gap Statistics
fviz_gap_stat(gap_stat)
Warning: `aes_string()` was deprecated in ggplot2 3.0.0.
ℹ Please use tidy evaluation idioms with `aes()`.
ℹ See also `vignette("ggplot2-in-packages")` for more information.
ℹ The deprecated feature was likely used in the factoextra package.
  Please report the issue at <https://github.com/kassambara/factoextra/issues>.

The gap statistic suggests that the best “number of clusters” is actually k = 1, meaning the data do not show strong cluster structure, so this dataset does not naturally separate into multiple clusters.

# Cut dendrogram into 2 cluster
hc_k <- cutree(hcl.complete, k = 2)
table(hc_k, exprset_final$wbc_group)
    
hc_k High WBC Low WBC
   1      102      95
   2        6       4

This table shows that after cutting the dendrogram into 2 clusters, almost all samples fall into the first cluster (197 out of 207), and only a very small group falls into the second cluster.

Summary: The hierarchical clustering does not form meaningful groups based on WBC. Almost all samples end up in the same large cluster, and the small cluster is mixed as well, and the optimal number of cluster is 1. This matches what we saw in PCA — WBC groups do not naturally separate based on global expression patterns.

The heatmap shows big gene-expression blocks, but the samples do not cluster by WBC level. High and Low WBC cases are mixed across the dendrogram, meaning there isn’t a strong global expression signature separating the two groups.

2. Kmeans Clustering

set.seed(42)
expr_mat_scale <- scale(t(expr_mat))
kmeans.2 <- kmeans(expr_mat_scale, centers = 2)

table(hc_k, kmeans.2$cluster)
    
hc_k   1   2
   1 175  22
   2   0  10
fviz_cluster(kmeans.2, data = expr_mat_scale)

The K-means results show that almost all samples were assigned to one large cluster, with only a small group of about ten samples forming a second cluster. The PCA plot confirms this pattern, where the two clusters overlap heavily in PC1–PC2 space and do not form clear, separated groups.

Differential Expression

library(limma)

Attaching package: 'limma'

    plotMA
# Set up the phenotype factor for WBC group
wbc_group <- pData(exprset_final)$wbc_group
wbc_group <- factor(wbc_group, levels = c("Low WBC", "High WBC"))

# Build design matrix
design <- model.matrix( ~ wbc_group - 1)
colnames(design)
[1] "wbc_groupLow WBC"  "wbc_groupHigh WBC"
colnames(design) <- c("LowWBC", "HighWBC")
head(design)
  LowWBC HighWBC
1      0       1
2      0       1
3      0       1
4      0       1
5      1       0
6      1       0
# Fit a linear regression
fit <- lmFit(expr_mat, design)

# Build contrast matrix
contrast.matrix <- makeContrasts( HighWBCvsLowWBC = HighWBC - LowWBC, levels=design)
contrast.matrix
         Contrasts
Levels    HighWBCvsLowWBC
  LowWBC               -1
  HighWBC               1
# Apply this contrast matrix to our fitted model
fit.contrasts <- contrasts.fit(fit, contrast.matrix)

# To adjust the standard deviations for each feature so that the t-test is moderated
eBayes.contrasts <- eBayes(fit.contrasts)

# To extract the comparison of interest,
final.result <- topTable(eBayes.contrasts, coef = "HighWBCvsLowWBC", number=dim(expr_mat)[1], sort.by="none")

# Count significant genes (FDR < 0.05)
sum(final.result$adj.P.Val < 0.05)
[1] 2981

# Get the gene symbol
gene.symbol <- getSYMBOL(featureNames(exprset_final), "hgu133plus2.db")

# Get the Entrez Gene ID
entrez <- getEG(featureNames(exprset_final), "hgu133plus2.db")


# Calculate the group mean
LowWBC<- rowMeans(exprs(exprset_final)[,pData(exprset_final)$wbc_group=="Low WBC"])
HighWBC<- rowMeans(exprs(exprset_final)[,pData(exprset_final)$wbc_group=="High WBC"])

# Check topTable results (in final.result) are in the same order as featureNames(exprset_final)
all.equal(rownames(final.result), featureNames(exprset_final))
[1] TRUE
# Create a data frame
report <- data.frame(gene.symbol, entrez, LowWBC, HighWBC, P.value = final.result$P.Value, FDR = final.result$adj.P.Val)

# To extract the rows having an FDR <= 0.05
sig_report <- report[report$FDR <0.05,] |> 
  filter(!(is.na(gene.symbol))) |> # remove those genes without the gene symbols as they may be control probe sets
  arrange(FDR)

Supervised Learning

1.Penalized Logistic Regression (lasso)

library(glmnet)
Loading required package: Matrix

Attaching package: 'Matrix'

# Fit lasso logistic regression
pData(exprset_final)$wbc_numeric <- ifelse(pData(exprset_final)$wbc_group=="High WBC", 1, 0) # recode to 1/0
lasso.wbc_group <- glmnet( x = t(expr_mat), y = pData(exprset_final)$wbc_numeric, family = binomial, alpha = 1)

### Identify the "best" model among all that were fit

# Define our predictive model as a function
lasso.fit <- function(x, y, lambda) {glmnet(x = x, y = y, lambda = lambda, family = binomial, alpha = 1)}

# Define a function the takes the model fit and outputs a prediction
lasso.predict <- function(fit, x) { predict(fit, newx = x, type = "response") }

# Calculate prediction mean squared error for each lambda
lasso.cv <- list()

for (s in 1:length(lasso.wbc_group$lambda)) {
  set.seed(4)
  lasso.cv[[s]]<-crossval(x =  t(expr_mat), y = pData(exprset_final)$wbc_numeric,
                          lambda = lasso.wbc_group$lambda[s], 
                          lasso.fit, lasso.predict, ngroup=10)
}

max(AUC)
[1] 0.9147026
# Plot the cross-validated AUC against lambda
plot(lasso.wbc_group$lambda, AUC)
# Extract the coefficient estimates for each value of lambda
coefficients <- coef(lasso.wbc_group)

# Extract our optimal model
final.model <- coefficients[,which.max(AUC)]

# Sum ofnon-zero coefficient estimates
sum(final.model!=0)
[1] 69

Using the lambda that gives the best cross-validated AUC, the lasso model keeps 69 non-zero coefficients. This means out of all ~9,700 filtered genes, only 69 genes carry most of the predictive signal for WBC level. So there is a relatively small gene subset that consistently helps distinguish children with high presenting WBC counts from those with low WBC counts.

# Fit the random forest
set.seed(42)
x <- t(expr_mat)   # samples × genes
y <- factor(pData(exprset_final)$wbc_group)

rf.wbc_group <- randomForest(
  x = x,
  y = y,
  importance = TRUE,
  ntree = 5000
)

# Produce a variable importance plot
varImpPlot(rf.wbc_group, type = 1, cex = 0.6)

The most important probe set is 205081_at based on the variable importance plot.

probeset <- "205081_at"
